```json
        "content": "\\FloatBarrier\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Cascade Ordering}\n\\label{sec:cascade}\n\nIf distributional collapse follows a predictable ordering across metrics, the earliest-responding metric can serve as a canary signal.\nWe formalize known metric properties---entropy as a continuous indicator of mode concentration, diversity as a threshold indicator of lexical coverage---under mode amplification~\\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale} (derivations in Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade_theory}).\n\n\\begin{designprinciple}[Entropy responds before diversity]\\label{thm:entropy_leads}\nUnder mode amplification: \\textup{(a)} entropy can decrease while the $\\epsilon$-effective support $S_\\epsilon(p) {=} |\\{i: p_i > \\epsilon\\}|$ (hence distinct-$n$) is unchanged; \\textup{(b)} if distinct-$n$ decreases, entropy has already decreased.\n\\end{designprinciple}\n\nThe asymmetry arises because entropy responds to any mass redistribution (first-order), while diversity changes only when a token probability crosses the generation threshold~$\\epsilon$; the margin $\\gamma = \\min_{i: p_i > \\epsilon}(p_i - \\epsilon)$ quantifies how much shift diversity absorbs silently.\n\n\\begin{designprinciple}[Diversity lag $\\geq 1$ generation]\\label{prop:div_lag}\nIf the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\\alpha\\|\\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\\|_\\infty < \\gamma(p_t, \\epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \\geq t_H + 1$.\n\\end{designprinciple}\n\nECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ECE} \\approx \\Delta C > 0$ whenever $\\Delta\\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade_theory}).\nThe predicted cascade $\\{H, \\mathrm{ECE}\\} \\to \\mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$ holds in 14/15 experiments at $\\alpha \\geq 0.30$: entropy and ECE onset at generation~1, distinct-$n$ at generation~2 ($t_H{=}t_{\\mathrm{ECE}}{=}1$, $t_D{=}2$ uniformly; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}; Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\nThe assumption requires monotonic entropy decline; SmolLM3-3B (3/8 at $\\alpha{=}1.0$; Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}) provides an empirical counter-example where entropy decouples from diversity, violating the ordering.\nThe cascade is also specific to diversity metrics: first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations entirely ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}).\n\n\\subsection{Design Considerations}\n\\label{sec:pitfalls}\n\nWe use Granger precedence testing~\\citep{granger1969investigating} to assess whether past canary values improve prediction of downstream metrics---not mechanistic causation; bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}.\nLead-lag analysis in iterative model collapse is fragile along three axes:\n\\emph{Method sensitivity}: five detection methods applied to identical data yield forward significance rates spanning 0\\% to 92\\%, with $\\kappa{=}0.185$.\n\\emph{Spurious regression}: first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$) while preserving canary$\\to$diversity (24/24$\\to$24/24)~\\citep{granger1974spurious}.\n\\emph{Regime dependence}: the same Granger test shifts from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_method_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Method sensitivity: five methods yield dramatically different FDR-significant rates on identical data. PermG achieves the lowest null-control FPR (6.7\\%, pooled across 10~seeds) with 75\\% detection at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$; dashed line: 5\\% nominal.}\n\\label{fig:method_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:mmct}\n\nWe compare five lead-lag detection methods---naive cross-correlation (NaiveXCorr), differenced cross-correlation (DiffXCorr), threshold onset, permutation Granger causality (PermG), and Toda--Yamamoto~\\citep{toda1995statistical}---in a multi-method comparison~\\citep{simonsohn2020specification} (algorithm in Appendix~\\ref{app:mmct}), calibrated against null-control ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) data.\nPooled null-control FPR across ten seeds (60 pairs): PermG 6.7\\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\\%, Threshold 24.1\\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\nPermG (4/60; 95\\% CI $[1.8\\%, 16.2\\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\\ref{app:shapley}).\nDetection results use equal-weight majority voting ($K{\\geq}3$ of 5 methods) with forward-direction filtering; $K{=}3$ yields 10.0\\% FPR with 75\\% true positive rate (TPR) at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:mmct_k_operating}).\nThe full MMCT procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:mmct}.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Multi-Method Consensus Test (MMCT)}\n\\label{alg:mmct}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\Require Time series pair $(X, Y)$; methods $\\{m_1, \\ldots, m_K\\}$ ($K{=}5$); null-control data $\\mathcal{N}$ (from $\\alpha{=}0.0$); tolerance $\\epsilon{=}0.01$\n\\Ensure $p$-value, consensus level $\\hat{K}$\n\\For{each method $m_k$}\n    \\State Compute empirical FPR: $\\widehat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k \\gets$ fraction of null-control pairs where $m_k$ rejects $H_0$\n    \\State Compute weight: $w_k \\gets 1 / \\max(\\widehat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k,\\; \\epsilon)$\n    \\State Apply $m_k$ to $(X, Y)$: $d_k \\gets \\mathbf{1}[m_k \\text{ rejects } H_0]$\n\\EndFor\n\\State Weighted consensus: $W(X {\\to} Y) \\gets \\sum_{k} w_k \\, d_k \\;/\\; \\sum_{k} w_k$\n\\State Build empirical null distribution of $W$ from all null-control pairs in $\\mathcal{N}$\n\\State Compute $p$-value from empirical null: $p \\gets \\Pr_{\\mathcal{N}}(W \\geq W_{\\mathrm{obs}})$\n\\State Consensus level: $\\hat{K} \\gets \\sum_{k} d_k$\n\\State \\Return $p$, $\\hat{K}$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nFPR-inverse weighting penalizes high-FPR methods on null-control data: naive cross-correlation (FPR${=}94.4\\%$) receives weight $w{=}1.06$, while PermG (FPR${=}6.7\\%$) receives $w{=}14.9$.\nThe empirical null distribution avoids parametric assumptions unreliable for short ($T{=}5$--$20$), non-stationary series.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation Granger causality.}\nPermG tests whether past values of canary metric $X$ improve prediction of downstream metric $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}, using 5{,}000 permutations~\\citep{phipson2010permutation} on first-differenced series with Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$ across 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs.\nThe permutation null controls Type~I error by construction; BH-FDR remains valid under positive regression dependence on a subset (PRDS)~\\citep{benjamini2001control}.\nEmpirically, 4/60 null-control rejections across ten seeds confirm adequate size control (8/10 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary}\n\\label{sec:theory}\n\nWe summarize the observed boundary as an empirical parametrization:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-\\gamma},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c}\n\\end{equation}\nUnder an empirical sub-linear signal model $S(\\alpha) = c \\cdot \\alpha^\\beta$ ($\\beta < 1$), the exponent satisfies $\\gamma = 1/(2\\beta)$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-1/(2\\beta)},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $A = (z^* \\sigma / c)^{1/\\beta}$ and $z^* = \\Phi^{-1}(\\pi^*)$ (justification in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nThis parametrization summarizes the observed pattern with three free parameters fitted to 3--4 data points; the wide $\\gamma$ confidence interval ($[0.64, 2.08]$) precludes distinguishing sub-linear from super-linear decay.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_detection_surface.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection rate surface over $\\alpha \\times T$ (PermG forward, 3~seeds). Dashed curve: fitted boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$. The blind zone ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$) shrinks as $T$ increases.}\n\\label{fig:detection_surface}\n\\end{figure}\n\nCross-$T$ validation yields $\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$ (profile 95\\% CI); the qualitative conclusion---detection improves with longer observation---is robust to the specific $\\gamma$ value (cross-validation details and shape analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nDense $\\alpha$ sampling favors a smooth sigmoid over a step function (Akaike information criterion difference $\\Delta\\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), but the fitted onset steepness ($k{=}15.7$) exceeds smooth-model predictions by $3$--$4{\\times}$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}).\n",
```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M~\\citep{biderman2023pythia} as the primary architecture.\nTraining data is drawn from WikiText-103~\\citep{merity2017pointer} following the replace paradigm of~\\citet{shumailov2024curse}: each generation fine-tunes on a mixture of $\\alpha$ synthetic and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ real data, generates 5{,}000 replacement samples, and fully replaces the synthetic pool (hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{app:training_details}).\nWe run $T{=}10$ generations across 13 contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, \\ldots, 1.00\\}$) with multi-seed replication, yielding ${\\sim}1{,}500$ statistical tests after BH-FDR correction~\\citep{benjamini1995controlling}.\nWe track two \\emph{canary metrics} (token entropy and ECE) and three \\emph{downstream metrics}: distinct-1, distinct-2~\\citep{li2016diversity}, and perplexity (distinct-3: Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}).\nPermG uses 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series~\\citep{shojaie2022granger} with maximum lag~5.\n\n\n\\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nDiversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\\%$ even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.25$.\nDespite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger detectability is sharply regime-dependent.\nPermG finds 0/8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 (Clopper--Pearson 95\\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead (shaded: inter-seed range).}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThis transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary ($C{=}99.75\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ vs.\\ $99.90\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.50$), yet detection jumps from null to reliable.\nSub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) confirm proximity to the boundary.\nSigmoid regression yields $\\alpha_c = 0.483 \\pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}); cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\\to}6/8$) and specification-invariance across 1{,}160 configurations (Appendix~\\ref{app:spec_curve}) argue for a genuine transition (Fisher exact: $p = 3.45 \\times 10^{-11}$).\n\n\\paragraph{Canary scoping.}\nFirst-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.\nCanary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \\to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$), while canary$\\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \\approx 0$, $p = 0.99$)~\\citep{granger1974spurious}.\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cascade_onset.pdf}\n\\caption{Cascade onset timeline (Pythia-410M, WikiText, fp32). Entropy and ECE reach onset at generation~1; distinct-$n$ follows at generation~2. The ordering $t_H \\leq t_D$ holds across all conditions.}\n\\label{fig:cascade_onset}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Blind zone.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null.\nBelow $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_spec_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{Specification curve (1{,}160 configurations). The blind$\\to$onset jump at $\\alpha_c{\\approx}0.50$ is robust across methods, pairs, and seeds.}\n\\label{fig:spec_curve}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection outcomes across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, $T{=}10$, fp32). PermG: forward FDR-significant pairs; $K{\\geq}3$: consensus pairs (of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).}\n\\label{tab:summary_detection}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & PermG & $K{\\geq}3$ & Regime \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & 0/8 & 0/8 & null \\\\\n0.25 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.45 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.50 & 6/8 & 6/8 & onset \\\\\n0.60 & 0/8 & 0/8 & anomaly \\\\\n0.75 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n1.00 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nThe PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\\emph{blind} ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$, 0/8), \\emph{onset} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \\emph{detection} ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\\alpha \\approx 0.60$ (0/8).\nThe blind zone exhibits zero detection across all seeds and methods below $\\alpha{=}0.45$, despite substantial diversity loss---a regime where collapse proceeds silently.\nOnset is remarkably steep: the sigmoid Hill coefficient $k{=}15.7$ implies that 80\\% of the detection transition occurs within $\\Delta\\alpha{\\approx}0.05$, approximately $3$--$4{\\times}$ steeper than smooth-signal predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}).\nAbove onset, the detection zone shows cross-seed consistency: all 3~fp32 seeds independently reach 6/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ and $\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$.\n\nAt $\\alpha{\\approx}0.60$, all direction-checked methods fail (PermG 0/8, threshold onset 0/8, Toda--Yamamoto 1/8), while bidirectional DiffXCorr maintains detection (6/8).\nThis pattern suggests that at intermediate contamination, entropy and diversity respond near-simultaneously, compressing the temporal lag below PermG's resolution at $T{=}10$.\nThe anomaly dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), consistent with $\\sqrt{T}$ power scaling restoring directional separation as additional observations accumulate (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_comparison}).\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}\n\\label{sec:t20}\n\nLonger observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\\alpha_c(5){\\geq}1.0$, $\\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling $\\alpha_c(T) \\propto T^{-\\gamma}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nProfile likelihood yields $\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$ (95\\% CI), confirming that detection improves with longer observation regardless of the specific exponent.\nThe mechanism is statistical power accumulation: each additional generation provides one more data point for the permutation test, and the signal-to-noise ratio grows as $\\sqrt{T}$, progressively resolving weaker contamination signals.\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.45$ column validates this prediction directly: undetectable at $T{\\leq}10$ (0/8), it crosses into reliable detection at $T{=}15$ (6/8, dual-seed) and remains stable at $T{=}20$ (5/8) and $T{=}25$ (6/8), precisely traversing the blind$\\to$detection boundary as the theory predicts.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response: PermG detection rate with sigmoid fit ($\\alpha_c{=}0.483$; shaded 95\\% CI). Regime bands mark blind, onset, sync-dip, and detection zones.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Evaluation at $\\alpha{=}1.0$}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nThe phase diagram (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}) characterizes detectability on Pythia-410M + WikiText-103; this section tests whether the detection signal generalizes across architectures at the most favorable contamination level ($\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nWe evaluate PermG across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families (345M--6.9B) and two domains (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_arch_table}).\nForward detection reaches ${\\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_arch_heatmap}).\n\n\\paragraph{Scale consistency.}\nThe Pythia-1.4B phase diagram qualitatively reproduces the 410M structure: detection boundary at $\\alpha_c{\\approx}0.50$, with cross-$T$ improvement at 3/4 $\\alpha$ values ($\\alpha{=}0.45$: $0/6{\\to}5/6$ from $T{=}10$ to $T{=}15$).\nThe 410M sync-dip at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ does not reproduce at 1.4B (4/6 at $T{=}10$), suggesting the anomaly is architecture-specific rather than a universal detection feature.\n\n\\paragraph{Failure mode taxonomy.}\nThe four non-confirming conditions each isolate a distinct PermG assumption violation.\n\\emph{Precision sensitivity}: GPT-2 bf16 yields 0/8 vs.\\ fp32 6/8, traced to bf16 quantization noise masking the entropy signal.\n\\emph{Bidirectional confounding}: GPT-2 s43 detects 6/8 in both forward and reverse directions, preventing directional assignment.\n\\emph{Seed dependence}: Pythia-1.4B shows s42 2/8 vs.\\ s43 6/8; directional asymmetry is seed-specific (s42 reverse 0/6 across all conditions; s43 reverse 6/6 at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.60$).\n\\emph{Entropy decoupling}: SmolLM3-3B reaches only 3/8, with ECE-only detection (entropy 0/4), violating the entropy-leads assumption of Design Principle~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads}.\nStandalone PermG claims therefore require architecture-specific null calibration; GPT-2 null FPR reaches 50\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}), driven by ${\\sim}4{\\times}$ stronger null drift than Pythia.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-architecture validation ($\\alpha{=}1.0$): PermG forward detection (${\\geq}6/8$) in 11/15 model-seed conditions spanning five families (345M--6.9B); faded bars: non-confirming conditions.}\n\\label{fig:cross_arch_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Canary-Based Intervention (Proof of Concept)}\n\\label{sec:intervention}\n\nAs a proof-of-concept under favorable conditions ($\\alpha{=}1.0$), we implement predictive cessation: training switches to pure real data when token entropy drops beyond a threshold from generation~0.\nThe 15\\% threshold lies within a robust band $[10\\%, 22\\%]$ where all thresholds trigger at the same generation (Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention_sensitivity}); thresholds below 10\\% risk false triggering from normal fine-tuning drift, while those above 22\\% delay intervention past the diversity-loss onset.\n\nThe predictive trigger fires at generation~1, one full generation before a reactive baseline that monitors distinct-1 directly.\nThe reactive baseline requires observing an actual diversity drop, which manifests only at generation~2 due to the cascade lag (Design Principle~\\ref{prop:div_lag}); by then, perplexity has already risen to $157.09$.\nPredictive cessation caps peak perplexity at $56.99$, a 64\\% reduction (Figure~\\ref{fig:intervention}).\nA naive PPL threshold also triggers at generation~1 due to the large PPL jump ($+113\\%$), but requires a held-out evaluation set; entropy is a zero-cost byproduct of generation (Appendix~\\ref{app:ppl_threshold}).\nThis advantage replicates on Pythia-1.4B with comparable effect size, confirming that the entropy-leads-diversity ordering transfers across scales within the Pythia family.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_intervention.pdf}\n\\caption{Canary-triggered intervention (Pythia-410M, $\\alpha{=}1.0$). Predictive cessation on entropy (generation~1) reduces peak perplexity by 64\\% versus reactive triggering on distinct-1 (generation~2).}\n\\label{fig:intervention}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Consensus threshold operating characteristics ($K \\in \\{2,3,4,5\\}$). FPR: null-control rate (10~seeds, 60~pairs; $K{\\geq}2$ uses 9-seed estimate). TPR: detection rate at selected $\\alpha$ levels (Pythia-410M, $T{=}10$, fp32). $K{=}3$ balances FPR control against detection power (75\\% at $\\alpha{=}1.0$); $K{=}4$ eliminates false positives but reduces TPR to ${\\leq}25\\%$.}\n\\label{tab:mmct_k_operating}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $K{\\geq}2$ & $K{\\geq}3$ & $K{\\geq}4$ & $K{=}5$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFPR ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) & 38.9\\% & 10.0\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.50$) & 85.4\\% & 66.7\\% & 25.0\\% & 4.2\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.75$) & 72.9\\% & 37.5\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) & 75.0\\% & 75.0\\% & 25.0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",
```

```json
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and generalizability.}\nOur landscape characterization uses Pythia-410M as the primary architecture; the phase diagram is Pythia-family-only, frontier-scale behavior (${>}$7B) is untested, and cross-architecture evaluation is limited to $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nAll experiments use full replacement with a single decoding strategy (temperature~$1.0$, top-$k{=}50$); data accumulation~\\citep{dey2024breaking} or lower temperatures would shift the landscape.\nMore fundamentally, the blind zone below $\\alpha_c$ means that substantial quality degradation (e.g., ${\\sim}40\\%$ distinct-1 decline at $\\alpha{=}0.25$) occurs before any statistical method signals detection.\nThe framework is therefore better characterized as a confirmation tool for ongoing collapse than an early-warning system.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical power.}\nPermG's empirical FPR is 6.7\\% (CI $[1.8\\%, 16.2\\%]$) and $K{\\geq}3$ consensus yields 10.0\\% (6/60); per-condition detection rates carry wide binomial CIs (e.g., $[0.25, 0.92]$ for 6/8), so the phase diagram represents a coarse topology rather than precise probability estimates.\n\n\\paragraph{Causal interpretation.}\nPermutation Granger testing establishes temporal precedence, not causation; our short-sequence regime ($T{\\leq}25$) limits statistical power relative to econometric standards ($T{\\geq}100$), and nonlinear alternatives to the linear PermG test yield no significant results in our setting (see \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 15
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/limitations.tex"
  },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "889a8ea0-fb05-4af3-ad0b-24a2e2af2e36",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\\citep{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nThis process can be triggered by vanishingly small synthetic fractions~\\citep{dohmatob2025strong}: lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the biases of its predecessors.\nAs machine-generated text grows prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production pipelines, and by the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.\n\nThis threat motivates a fundamental question: \\emph{can we reliably detect the onset of model collapse?}\nThe answer is less clear than it might appear.\nIterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points) where detection power drops sharply~\\citep{boettiger2012limits}, and model collapse is a first-order transition~\\citep{truong2026phase}, structurally ruling out the critical-slowing-down early-warning signals that ecologists rely on~\\citep{scheffer2009early} (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\nMore fundamentally, no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem; as we show, the choice of statistical test alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null on identical data.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_phase_diagram.pdf}\n\\caption{Phase diagram (Pythia-410M, fp32, PermG). FDR-significant canary$\\to$diversity pairs across $\\alpha$ and $T$; dashed curve: fitted boundary (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}); $\\diamond$: non-monotonic anomaly; $\\star$: dissolution at $T{=}25$.}\n\\label{fig:phase_diagram}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe systematically map this landscape on Pythia-410M~\\citep{biderman2023pythia} across 13 contamination levels with multi-seed replication (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}).\nOur most consequential finding concerns method sensitivity: five statistical methods applied to identical data yield null-control FPR spanning 7\\% to 94\\% ($\\kappa{=}0.185$; Figure~\\ref{fig:method_comparison}), demonstrating that single-method analyses cannot reliably distinguish real signals from artifacts.\nPermutation Granger causality (PermG), the lowest-FPR method (6.7\\%, CI $[1.8\\%, 16.2\\%]$, 10~seeds), reveals structured terrain rather than smooth degradation: detection is absent through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}), before a non-monotonic dip and eventual reliable detection above an empirical boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}).\nCross-architecture evaluation at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ across five families (345M--6.9B) supports forward detection in 11/15 conditions, with four non-confirming cases isolating distinct failure modes (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nOur canary claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$~\\citep{li2016diversity}); Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not causation (\\S\\ref{sec:method}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{We document method sensitivity as a primary finding} (\\S\\ref{sec:mmct}).\n    Five methods yield null-control FPR from 7\\% to 94\\% ($\\kappa{=}0.185$); PermG, the lowest-FPR method across all five (6.7\\%, CI $[1.8\\%, 16.2\\%]$, 10~seeds), is selected as the primary detector.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{We map the detectability landscape} of iterative model collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}--\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\n    Detection is absent through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 within 2 percentage points; a detection boundary parametrized as $\\alpha_c(T) \\propto T^{-\\gamma}$ ($\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$; Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}) organizes three confirmed regimes (blind, onset, detection) plus one unexplained non-monotonic anomaly.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{We empirically validate a cascade ordering for canary metric selection} (\\S\\ref{sec:cascade}).\n    Under mode amplification, entropy responds before diversity by ${\\geq}1$ generation (Principles~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads}--\\ref{prop:div_lag}), confirmed in 14/15 experiments ($\\alpha \\geq 0.30$; GPT-2-medium s43 bidirectional).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 37,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 37
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },
```

```text
1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Training Details and Metric Drift}
4	\label{app:training_details}
5	\label{app:drift}
6	
7	\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}
8	Each generation fine-tunes for 5{,}000 steps using AdamW (learning rate $5 \times 10^{-5}$, 500 warmup steps, weight decay $0.01$, batch size 4 for Pythia-410M, 32 for GPT-2-medium).
9	Generation uses temperature $1.0$, top-$k{=}50$, max length 512 tokens, producing 5{,}000 samples per generation from 5{,}000-sample subsets of WikiText-103.
10	Canary metrics: token entropy $H(p_\theta | \text{context})$ over 1{,}000 held-out samples; ECE with 15 bins.
11	Additional downstream metrics (distinct-3, effective rank, Self-BLEU) appear in subsequent appendix sections.
12	
13	\paragraph{Metric drift.}
14	
15	\begin{table}[h]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\caption{Metric drift (\%, generation~0$\to$10) for Pythia-410M across contamination levels. Diversity collapse occurs at all $\alpha$ levels, with the strongest decline at low-to-moderate contamination ($\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-1 $-40.3{\pm}0.3\%$) rather than full replacement ($\alpha{=}1.0$: $-27.8{\pm}1.6\%$). $^\dagger$$\alpha{=}0.10$: single seed (seed~42, preliminary). $\alpha{=}0.00$, $\alpha{=}0.25$, $\alpha{=}0.50$, $\alpha{=}0.75$: 3-seed mean${\pm}$std (seeds 42, 43, 44); $\alpha{=}1.0$: 4-seed mean${\pm}$std (seeds 42, 43, 44, 45).}
19	\label{tab:drift_summary}
20	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%

22	\toprule
23	$\alpha$ & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perplexity \\

25	0.00 & $-4.6{\pm}0.4$ & $+37.6{\pm}14$ & $-14.1{\pm}0.4$ & $-9.6{\pm}0.4$ & $-20.6{\pm}1.0$ \\
26	0.10$^\dagger$ & $-5.3$ & $+57$ & $-39.6$ & $-56.4$ & $-21.0$ \\
27	0.25 & $-4.7{\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\pm}0.7$ & $-18.1{\pm}0.8$ \\
28	0.50 & $-18.0{\pm}0.5$ & $+822.6{\pm}18.3$ & $-37.1{\pm}0.4$ & $-50.4{\pm}1.0$ & $+21.0{\pm}0.1$ \\
29	0.75 & $-27.6{\pm}0.2$ & $+1791{\pm}18$ & $-32.3{\pm}0.5$ & $-41.0{\pm}1.0$ & $+182.6{\pm}0.7$ \\

36	\section{Distinct-3 Results}
37	\label{app:distinct3}
38	
39	Table~\ref{tab:distinct3_drift} reports distinct-3 drift across all contamination levels.
40	Distinct-3 exhibits the largest absolute decline among diversity metrics at low contamination ($-58.9{\pm}0.9\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.25$) but attenuates at higher levels ($-30.5\%$ at $\alpha{=}1.0$), consistent with the diversity--calibration dissociation discussed in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
41	
42	\begin{table}[h]
43	\centering
44	\small
45	\caption{Distinct-3 drift (\%, generation~0$\to$10) across contamination levels. $\alpha{=}0.00$, $\alpha{=}0.25$, $\alpha{=}0.75$: 3-seed mean${\pm}$std; $\alpha{=}1.0$: 4-seed mean${\pm}$std; $\alpha{=}0.50$: single seed.}
46	\label{tab:distinct3_drift}
47	\begin{tabular}{lc}
48	\toprule
49	$\alpha$ & Distinct-3 drift (\%) \\

59	
60	Distinct-3 exhibits higher lag instability than distinct-1/2 (bimodal lag distribution, std${>}1.5$ vs.\ ${<}0.5$ generations), reflecting its lower signal-to-noise ratio at 512-token generation length.
61	
62	
63	\section{Observation Window Sensitivity}
64	\label{app:window_sensitivity}
65	
66	Consensus classification stabilizes at $T_{\min}{\geq}5$ (Table~\ref{tab:window_sensitivity}): all six effect-size reference pairs reach $K{\geq}4/5$ agreement at every window from $T_{\min}{=}5$ through $T_{\min}{=}10$.

PermG forward detection (6/8) and consensus classification ($K{\geq}4$) both stabilize at $T_{\min}{\geq}5$, with no significance flips for $T_{\min} \in [5, 10]$. This robustness confirms that the choice of $T_{\min}{=}5$ in the main analysis does not drive results.}

TY pairs: number of effect-size reference pairs (out of 6) reaching Toda--Yamamoto significance (moderate supplementary method; \S\ref{sec:method}). Consensus stabilizes at $T_{\min}{\geq}5$; TY stabilizes at $T_{\min}{\geq}6$.}

95	
96	\section{Robustness Analysis}
97	\label{app:robustness}
98	\label{app:spec_curve}
99	
100	\paragraph{Permutation count and FDR sensitivity.}
101	PermG $p$-values are stable between 5{,}000 and 10{,}000 permutations (max $|\Delta p|{=}0.010$, 0/24 significance flips at $\alpha{=}0.75$).
102	Treatment/null separation is robust to FDR threshold choice: at $q{=}0.05$, 4/36 null pairs vs.\ 19/24 treatment pairs reach significance; at $q{=}0.10$, null rejections rise to 8/36.
103	At the onset boundary ($\alpha{=}0.45$), minimum FDR-corrected $p$-values ($0.059$--$0.091$) are consistent with a continuous power transition rather than binary absence of signal.
104	
105	\paragraph{Specification curve.}
106	We construct 1{,}160 specifications from 13 contamination levels, 4~seeds, and 8 canary$\to$downstream pairs (all PermG, $n_\text{perm}{=}5{,}000$, BH FDR $q{=}0.05$).
107	
108	\begin{table}[h]
109	\centering
110	\small
111	\caption{Specification curve summary (1{,}160 specifications, D120-compliant). The blind$\to$onset jump is robust across specifications: 0\% significance rate through $\alpha{=}0.45$, rising to 75\% at $\alpha{=}0.50$.}
112	\label{tab:spec_curve}
113	\begin{tabular}{lc}
114	\toprule
115	Metric & Value \\
116	\midrule
117	Total specifications & 1{,}160 \\
118	Significant (FDR $q{<}0.05$) & 536 (46.2\%) \\
119	\midrule
120	\emph{By regime} & \\
121	\quad Blind ($\alpha \leq 0.45$) & 0\% \\
122	\quad Onset ($\alpha{=}0.50$) & 75\% \\
123	\quad Detection ($\alpha \geq 0.75$) & 62--75\% \\
124	\bottomrule
125	\end{tabular}
126	\end{table}
127	
128	The blind$\to$onset jump holds across the vast majority of specifications (Figure~\ref{fig:spec_curve}).
129	
130	\begin{figure}[h]
131	\centering
132	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_spec_curve.pdf}
133	\caption{Specification curve across 1{,}160 PermG configurations (13 $\alpha$ levels $\times$ 4~seeds $\times$ 8~pairs, Pythia-410M, fp32). Each dot represents one specification's $p$-value; specifications are sorted by $\alpha$ level. The sharp transition from non-significant (above dashed line) to significant (below) at $\alpha{=}0.50$ is invariant across seeds and metric pairs, confirming that the detection onset is a robust property of the data rather than an artifact of a particular analytical choice.}
134	\label{fig:spec_curve_app}
135	\end{figure}
136	
137	
138	\section{Transfer Entropy and Change-Point Analysis}
139	\label{app:transfer_entropy}
140	\phantomsection\label{app:te_mi}
141	
142	Transfer entropy (TE; \citealp{schreiber2000measuring}; 3-bin discretization, $n{=}5{,}000$ permutations) yields 0/192 BH-corrected significant pairs across all conditions, confirming that nonparametric information-theoretic methods lack power at $T{=}11$.
143	A KNN-based TE estimator ($k{=}5$) shows forward-dominant TE fraction increasing monotonically with $\alpha$ (4/8 at $\alpha{=}0.25$ to 8/8 at $\alpha{=}1.0$), but this does not reach statistical significance after FDR correction.
144	
145	\phantomsection\label{app:change_point}
146	\paragraph{Change-point detection.}
147	Max-split change-point tests ($n{=}5{,}000$ bootstrap) yield 66.7\% FPR under null control (12/18, $\alpha{=}0.00$), precluding use as a standalone detector.
148	However, the temporal ordering of change-points reverses between null and treatment: under null, downstream metrics break first (gen~2) before canary (gen~4--8); under treatment ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$), canary breaks first (gen~1) before downstream (gen~2--4).
149	This ordering reversal shifts monotonically with $\alpha$, consistent with the Granger-precedence analysis.
150	
151	
152	\section{Sequential Monitoring: CUSUM vs Calibrated Threshold}
153	\label{app:csmp}
154	
155	CUSUM sequential testing~\citep{page1954continuous} calibrated against $\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (FAR${=}$0\%) produces identical alarm times as a simple $2\sigma$ threshold at all tested operating points: both trigger at generation~1 for $\alpha{\geq}0.50$.
156	The equivalence arises because the generation-0$\to$1 entropy drop ($|\Delta x|{>}0.7$) greatly exceeds null variability ($\sigma_0{\approx}0.03$); CUSUM's cumulative memory provides no advantage when the initial shift dominates.
157	
158	\section{Background and Notation}
159	\label{app:background}
160	\label{sec:background}
161	
162	This section establishes the formal setup and notation used throughout the paper.
163	
164	\paragraph{Iterative training loop.}
165	Let $M_0$ denote a pretrained language model and $\mathcal{D}_\text{real}$ a fixed corpus of human-written text.
166	At each generation $t \geq 1$, the model $M_{t-1}$ generates a synthetic corpus $\mathcal{D}_\text{synth}^{(t-1)}$, and the next-generation model $M_t$ is fine-tuned on a mixture of $\alpha$ fraction synthetic data and $(1{-}\alpha)$ fraction real data:
167	\begin{equation}
168	    \mathcal{D}_\text{train}^{(t)} = \alpha \cdot \mathcal{D}_\text{synth}^{(t-1)} \;+\; (1{-}\alpha) \cdot \mathcal{D}_\text{real}, \quad \alpha \in [0, 1].
169	    \label{eq:mixing}
170	\end{equation}
171	At $\alpha{=}1.0$, this reduces to the full-replacement paradigm of \citet{shumailov2024curse}.
172	
173	\paragraph{Metric families.}
174	\emph{Canary metrics}: token entropy ($H(p_\theta | \text{context})$ on held-out samples) and expected calibration error (ECE).
175	\emph{Downstream metrics}: distinct-$n$ ($n \in \{1, 2, 3\}$)~\citep{li2016diversity} and perplexity.
176	Each metric produces a time series of $T{+}1$ observations.
177	
178	\paragraph{Lead-lag testing.}
179	Given canary series $X$ and downstream series $Y$, the central question is whether changes in $X$ temporally precede changes in $Y$, requiring directionality and confounder control beyond simple correlation.
180	We employ five methods spanning permissive to conservative: naive cross-correlation, differenced cross-correlation, threshold onset detection, permutation Granger causality~\citep{shojaie2022granger}, and Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality~\citep{toda1995statistical}.
181

233	\paragraph{Calibration scope.}
234	All calibration results are specific to our setup (Pythia-410M, WikiText-103, $T{=}10$).
235	On C4 at $\alpha{=}0.50$, PermG reverse reaches 7/8 (seed-dependent: s43 1/8), indicating directional asymmetry should be verified per domain.
236	Token entropy ($H(p_\theta|\text{context})$ on held-out data) and perplexity ($\exp(H_\text{cross})$ on generated text) co-move synchronously, explaining why first-differencing eliminates canary$\to$perplexity but preserves canary$\to$diversity.

322	\section{Cross-Seed Consistency at $\alpha{=}0.60$}
323	\label{app:alpha060}
324	
325	The non-monotonic detection gap at $\alpha{=}0.60$ (\S\ref{sec:dose_response}) is consistent across seeds: PermG yields 0/8 FDR-significant forward pairs for both seed~42 (v4 recompilation) and seed~43.
326	An earlier pipeline version (v3, seed~42) reported 3/8; the discrepancy is resolved by the d120 recompilation that corrected FPR calibration.
327	The sigmoid fit excluding $\alpha{=}0.60$ yields $k{=}5.66$, $\alpha_c{=}0.765$, $R^2{=}0.286$; including it degrades $R^2$ to 0.205 and shifts $\alpha_c$ rightward by 0.058, confirming the point is a genuine outlier rather than noise.
328	
329	\section{PPL Threshold Baseline}
330	\label{app:ppl_threshold}
331	
332	A reviewer asked whether a naive perplexity threshold could match entropy's detection timing.
333	We evaluate thresholds at $+10\%$, $+25\%$, $+50\%$, and $+100\%$ above the generation-0 baseline PPL (26.9).
334	All thresholds trigger at generation~1, the same generation as entropy onset, because the gen-0$\to$gen-1 PPL jump is $+113\%$ (26.9$\to$57.4).
335	Only thresholds above $+113\%$ delay triggering to generation~2.
336	Entropy's advantage over PPL monitoring is therefore operational, not temporal: entropy is a zero-cost byproduct of generation (computed from the model's output distribution during decoding), requiring no held-out evaluation set, while PPL requires a separate clean test set and a full forward pass.
337	Entropy also detects distributional narrowing (mode collapse) directly, while PPL detects its downstream consequence (degraded generalization).
338	
339	
340	\section{PermG Power Analysis}
341	\label{app:power_analysis}
342	
343	We simulate PermG power under a bivariate DGP with linear coupling $\beta$ ($T \in \{6,\ldots,20\}$, 100 replications, 5{,}000 permutations, BH FDR $q{=}0.05$).
344	At $T{=}10$, $\beta_{50}{=}2.0$ (50\% power requires strong coupling); at $T{=}20$, $\beta_{50}$ drops to 0.9 (Table~\ref{tab:beta50}).
345	Power grows faster than $\sqrt{T}$, so the $\sqrt{T}$ formula in \S\ref{sec:theory} is a conservative lower bound.
346	FPR under FDR correction remains ${\leq}2\%$ across all $T$.

367	\section{Non-Stationarity Robustness Analysis}
368	\label{app:nonstationarity}
369	
370	Monte Carlo simulation (AR(1) DGP, $T{=}11$, 1{,}000 reps) shows PermG FPR inflates to ${\sim}10\%$ under strong non-stationarity ($\varphi{\geq}0.95$), while first-differencing maintains proper size ($4.2$--$6.6\%$) at the cost of reduced power (Table~\ref{tab:nonstationarity_sim}).
371	Our pipeline applies first-differencing before permutation testing, combining both advantages.
372	Under null conditions ($\alpha{=}0.0$), token entropy exhibits $\varphi{=}-0.51$ (far from the danger zone); first-differencing reduces distinct-1's high raw $\varphi$ ($0.81$--$0.97$, reflecting deterministic trend) to $-0.14$--$0.44$.
373	Our empirical FPR of 4/60 (6.7\%) confirms adequate size control.
374

Null token entropy exhibits negative autocorrelation ($\varphi{=}-0.51$), far from the non-stationarity danger zone ($\varphi{\geq}0.95$). Treatment series show higher persistence in raw levels but first-differencing reduces all coefficients below 0.44, confirming that our pipeline controls for non-stationarity.}
379	\label{fig:ar1_diagnostic}
380	\end{figure}

498	\section{Sigmoid Transition Shape}
499	\label{app:sigmoid_shape}
500	
501	Sigmoid regression on the original 13-point $T{=}10$ grid yields $\alpha_c = 0.483 \pm 0.006$ but $k = 478 \pm 475$ ($\mathrm{SE}(k) \approx k$), indicating that the coarse grid cannot constrain the transition shape.
502	Dense $\alpha$ sampling (17~values at $T{=}10$) resolves this ambiguity: formal likelihood-ratio testing strongly favors a smooth sigmoid over a literal step function ($\Delta\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), with fitted steepness $k{=}15.7$ (95\% CI $[9.4, 28.2]$).
503	The transition is steep but finite-width, not a literal discontinuity.
504	This observed steepness exceeds what smooth signal models predict: VAR(1) simulations under H0 (linear, $k{=}6.1$) and H0$'$ (concave, $k{=}4.9$) produce onset sigmoids with $k{=}4.9$--$6.1$ (\S\ref{app:power_sim}), yielding steepness ratios $k_{\mathrm{obs}} / k_{\mathrm{H0}} \approx 2.6$--$3.2$ (CI lower bound ratio ${\approx}2$).
505	We do not fit a combined $T{=}10 + T{=}20$ sigmoid because $\alpha{=}0.45$ yields contradictory counts under the two sequence lengths (0/8 vs.\ 5/8), reflecting genuine power dependence on $T$ rather than a single dose-response curve.
506	
507	
508	\begin{figure}[h]
509	\centering
510	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/differencing_dmax.pdf}
511	\caption{First-differencing order selection diagnostic. The optimal differencing order $d_{\max}$ is evaluated via ADF test statistics across all canary$\to$downstream series at $\alpha{=}1.0$. First-order differencing ($d{=}1$) achieves stationarity in all series (ADF $p{<}0.05$), confirming that higher-order differencing is unnecessary and would incur additional power loss.}
512	\label{fig:differencing_dmax}
513	\end{figure}

628	\section{Cascade Ordering Theory: Derivations}
629	\label{app:cascade_theory}
630	
631	This section provides the full derivations for Design Principle~\ref{thm:entropy_leads} and Design Principle~\ref{prop:div_lag} from \S\ref{sec:cascade}, plus supporting results.
632	
633	\begin{assumption}[Mode Amplification]\label{ass:mode_amp}
634	The training map $\mathcal{T}$ redistributes probability mass from low-probability to high-probability tokens: there exists a threshold $\bar{p}$ such that $\mathcal{T}(p)_i > p_i \Rightarrow p_i \geq \bar{p}$ and $\mathcal{T}(p)_j < p_j \Rightarrow p_j \leq \bar{p}$.
635	\end{assumption}
636	
637	This captures the standard finite-capacity approximation effect: MLE on finite samples from a peaked distribution amplifies modes and suppresses the tail~\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale}.
638	
639	\begin{proposition}[Entropy is a first-order indicator]\label{prop:entropy_first}
640	Let $p \in \Delta^{V-1}$ and $\delta = \alpha(\mathcal{T}(p) - p)$ be a mode-amplifying perturbation with $\sum_i \delta_i = 0$. Then $\Delta H = H(p+\delta) - H(p) = -\sum_i \delta_i \log p_i + O(\|\delta\|^2) < 0$.
641	\end{proposition}
642	
643	\begin{proof}
644	Taylor-expanding $H$ around $p$:
645	$H(p+\delta) = H(p) - \sum_i \delta_i \log p_i - \sum_i \delta_i - \frac{1}{2}\sum_i \delta_i^2/p_i + O(\|\delta\|^3)$.
646	Since $\sum_i \delta_i = 0$: $\Delta H = -\sum_i \delta_i \log p_i - \frac{1}{2}\sum_i \delta_i^2/p_i + O(\|\delta\|^3)$.
647	Under mode amplification, tokens gaining mass ($\delta_i > 0$) have $p_i \geq \bar{p}$ (hence $\log p_i \geq \log \bar{p}$) and tokens losing mass ($\delta_j < 0$) have $p_j \leq \bar{p}$ (hence $\log p_j \leq \log \bar{p}$).
648	Writing $m = \sum_{i:\delta_i > 0} \delta_i$: $-\sum_i \delta_i \log p_i = -m[\overline{\log p}^{+} - \overline{\log p}^{-}] < 0$, where $\overline{\log p}^{\pm}$ are the $|\delta|$-weighted means over gaining/losing tokens.
649	The second-order Fisher information term $-\frac{1}{2}\sum_i \delta_i^2/p_i$ is always negative, reinforcing the decrease.
650	\end{proof}
651	
652	\begin{proposition}[Diversity is a threshold indicator]\label{prop:div_threshold}
653	Define the $\epsilon$-effective support $S_\epsilon(p) = |\{i \in [V]: p_i > \epsilon\}|$, where $\epsilon \approx c/N$ is the generation threshold for sample size $N$.
654	Under the same perturbation $\delta$: $\Delta S_\epsilon = -|\{i: p_i > \epsilon \text{ and } p_i + \delta_i \leq \epsilon\}|$.
655	This equals zero whenever $\|\delta\|_\infty < \gamma(p,\epsilon) := \min_{i: p_i > \epsilon}(p_i - \epsilon)$.
656	\end{proposition}
657	
658	\begin{proof}
659	Token $i$ exits the effective support iff $p_i > \epsilon$ and $p_i + \delta_i \leq \epsilon$, requiring $|\delta_i| \geq p_i - \epsilon$.
660	If $\|\delta\|_\infty < \gamma$, no token satisfies this condition.
661	\end{proof}
662	
663	\paragraph{Derivation of Design Principle~\ref{thm:entropy_leads}.}
664	\emph{Part (a):} Take any mode-amplifying $\delta$ with $\|\delta\|_\infty < \gamma(p,\epsilon)$.
665	By Proposition~\ref{prop:div_threshold}, $\Delta S_\epsilon = 0$.
666	By Proposition~\ref{prop:entropy_first}, $\Delta H < 0$.
667	Such $\delta$ exists for all $\alpha < \gamma / \max_i |\mathcal{T}(p)_i - p_i|$.
668	\emph{Part (b):} If $\Delta S_\epsilon < 0$, some token $k$ has $p_k > \epsilon$ but $p_k + \delta_k \leq \epsilon$, so $\delta_k < 0$.
669	Under mode amplification, this implies $p_k \leq \bar{p}$; the total mass transferred to high-probability tokens ensures $\overline{\log p}^+ > \overline{\log p}^-$.
670	By Proposition~\ref{prop:entropy_first}, $\Delta H < 0$. $\square$
671	
672	\paragraph{Derivation of Design Principle~\ref{prop:div_lag}.}
673	By the margin condition, at every generation $s \leq t_H$ the perturbation norm $\alpha\|\mathcal{T}(p_s) - p_s\|_\infty$ is less than $\gamma(p_s, \epsilon)$.
674	By Proposition~\ref{prop:div_threshold}, no token exits the effective support at any $s \leq t_H$, so $\Delta S_\epsilon = 0$ for all $s \leq t_H$.
675	Since time is discrete, $t_D \geq t_H + 1$. $\square$
676	
677	\paragraph{ECE co-movement (qualitative argument).}
678	Under conditional mode amplification (peakier $q_\theta(\cdot|x)$ at each generation), per-token conditional entropy $h(x) = H(q_\theta(\cdot|x))$ decreases while confidence $c(x) = \max_y q_\theta(y|x) \geq \exp(-h(x))$ increases.
679	Averaging: $\bar{H}_t$ decreases, $C_t$ increases.
680	With accuracy approximately stationary in early collapse ($A_t \approx A_0$), $\Delta\mathrm{ECE} \approx \Delta C_t > 0$ whenever $\Delta\bar{H}_t < 0$.
681	This argument establishes the direction of co-movement; the quantitative relationship depends on distribution geometry.
682	$\bar{H}_t$ denotes mean conditional entropy $\mathbb{E}_{x \sim p_{\text{eval}}}[h(x)]$, which differs from the marginal entropy $H_t = -\sum_i p_t(i)\log p_t(i)$ used in Design Principle~\ref{thm:entropy_leads}; under mode amplification, both decrease monotonically, so the cascade ordering is consistent across both formulations.
683	
684	\paragraph{Empirical support.}
685	Cascade ordering $t_H \leq t_D$ holds in 14/15 experiments at $\alpha \geq 0.30$ (GPT-2-medium s43: bidirectional).
686	Table~\ref{tab:cascade_onset} reports onset generations for representative conditions.
687	
688	\begin{table}[h]
689	\centering
690	\small
691	\caption{Cascade onset generations (5\% threshold) for representative experiments (Pythia-410M, WikiText, fp32). Phase~1: entropy and ECE ($t_H$, $t_{\mathrm{ECE}}$); Phase~2: diversity ($t_D$, min over distinct-$n$); Phase~3: MAUVE ($t_M$). All conditions confirm the predicted ordering $t_H = t_{\mathrm{ECE}} \leq t_D \leq t_M$.}
692	\label{tab:cascade_onset}
693	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
694	\toprule
695	Seed & $\alpha$ & $t_H$ & $t_{\mathrm{ECE}}$ & $t_D$ & $t_M$ \\

697	42 & 0.50 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 3 \\
698	43 & 0.50 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 2 \\
699	42 & 0.75 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 3 \\
700	43 & 0.75 & 1 & 1 & 2 & 2 \\

705	
706	\phantomsection\label{app:alpha_c_proof}
707	\paragraph{Justification of the empirical boundary model.}
708	At the detection threshold, $\mathrm{Power}(\alpha_c, T) = \pi^*$.
709	Since $\alpha_c$ lies below the non-monotonic dip region ($\alpha_c < 0.55$), the signal model applies directly.
710	From the power function $\mathrm{Power} = \Phi(S(\alpha) \cdot \sqrt{T} / \sigma)$, setting $S(\alpha_c) \cdot \sqrt{T} / \sigma = z^*$ and substituting the signal model $S(\alpha) = c\,\alpha^\beta$ yields $c\,\alpha_c^\beta = z^* \sigma / \sqrt{T}$.
711	Solving: $\alpha_c(T) = (z^* \sigma / c)^{1/\beta} \cdot T^{-1/(2\beta)}$, giving the stated form with exponent $\gamma = 1/(2\beta)$.
712	
713	\paragraph{Calibration and consistency.}
714	Fitting $P(\mathrm{det}|\alpha,T) = \Phi(A \cdot \alpha^\beta \cdot \sqrt{T} - z_0)$ to $T \in \{10, 20\}$ yields $\beta{=}0.24$, $\alpha_\infty{=}0$.
715	Held-out test ($T \in \{5, 15\}$): MAE${=}0.106$, 95\% CI coverage 6/6.
716	Leave-one-$T$-out CV (15 cells, sync-dip excluded): MAE${=}0.037$, coverage 15/15.
717	Profile likelihood yields $\hat{\beta}{=}0.66$ (CI $[0.24, 0.78]$); the wide interval reflects a flat surface.
718	
719	\section{Power Simulation: Shape Comparison}
720	\label{app:power_sim}
721	
722	Simulated PermG detection under linear (H0), concave (H0$'$), and step (H1) signal models (1{,}000 reps, $n_\text{perm}{=}1{,}000$, BH-FDR $q{=}0.05$).
723	Smooth models produce onset steepness $k{=}4.9$--$6.1$; the observed $k{=}15.7$ is ${\approx}3{\times}$ higher.
724	The scaling exponent alone cannot distinguish smooth from step boundaries (H0$'$ $\gamma{=}1.87$ vs.\ observed $\gamma{=}2.08$).
725	The 6/8 detection ceiling reflects MAUVE floor saturation, not signal limitations.
726	
727	\begin{figure}[h]
728	\centering
729	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/permg_power_curve.pdf}
730	\caption{PermG power curves as a function of coupling strength $\beta$ for $T \in \{6, 8, 10, 15, 20\}$ (100 replications, 5{,}000 permutations, BH-FDR $q{=}0.05$). Power increases faster than $\sqrt{T}$, confirming that the theoretical $\sqrt{T}$ formula is a conservative lower bound. At $T{=}10$, 50\% power requires $\beta{\geq}2.0$; at $T{=}20$, this drops to $\beta{\approx}0.9$.}
731	\label{fig:permg_power_curve}
732	\end{figure}

734	\section{T=20 Extension Results}
735	\label{app:t20_comparison}
736	
737	At $\alpha{\geq}0.50$, PermG counts are consistent across $T{=}10$ and $T{=}20$ (6/8 at both), confirming signal-structure determination.
738	At $\alpha{=}0.45$, the shift from 0/8 ($T{=}10$) to 5/8 ($T{=}20$) supports power-limited (Model~B) over structurally-undetectable (Model~A), lowering $\alpha_c$ below 0.45.
739	The $\alpha{=}0.60$ dip persists at $T{=}20$ but dissolves at $T{=}25$ (5/8), confirming $\sqrt{T}$ power scaling eventually overcomes it.
740	Directional selectivity is consistent: forward 6/8, reverse 0/8 at $\alpha \in \{0.50, 0.75, 1.0\}$ across all tested $T$.
```
